## 4.3 Speech translation technology: an overview

Automatic speech translation is a cluster of three major technologies in the NLP domain. The overall process requires the understanding of spoken utterance followed by its translation into the target language, consistent with the language grammar rules. This complete process would create a pathway for patients with limited knowledge of the language at the service provider. It can be achieved by sequence execution of three technologies, that is, ASR, machine translation (MT), and TTS. The pipeline is depicted in [Figure 4.3](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_003). This technological cluster will also help the patients with visual or any physical impairments to communicate with the doctors or the care providers as well as to connect with the digital world to take a more direct role in self-health and treatment. Spoken language communication needs a system that can recognize, interpret, and respond.

![Figure 4.3: Pipeline architecture of speech-to-speech translation.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_003.png)

Figure 4.3: Pipeline architecture of speech-to-speech translation.

### 4.3.1 Speech production and perception

One of the major concerns in the field of automatic speech processing is the gap between the performance of machine and human. Incorporating knowledge into the machine about how humans produce and perceive sounds will help improve the performance of a machine. Before moving into the details of using technology to remove the language barrier, the study of the anatomy of speech production must be understood. Automating the whole process of speech processing and understanding is a complicated task, not only due to domain complexities but also due to the complexities associated with the vocal machine of the human system. The system will require handling the vocal tract (VT) characteristics of individual, accent due to nativity, background noise, and also the microphone characteristics in addition to coping with the ambiguity of the language and nongrammaticality of spoken conversations.

Human voice production is a complex system that is capable of producing several distinct sounds. Lungs, larynx, and VT combine to constitute the human voice production system. VT, that is, oral/nasal cavity, soft/hard palate, tongue, lips, jaw, and teeth form the most important organ affecting the speech production. The respiratory (lungs) and the laryngeal (larynx) subsystems provide the source (air pressure) for speech signal. The articulatory subsystem works upon the generated signal to produce different phones. These sound units can be categorized as voice, unvoiced, or plosives depending upon the articulators responsible for their production. The varying nature of sound units creates challenges for their automated processing.

Speech perception is the other vertical of spoken language understanding. It has been shown that acoustic speech signal does not directly map to the phonological segments ([Casserly et al., 2010](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_007)). On the other hand, the human auditory system is a combination and transformation of hearing and voice that are inseparable. The inherent connection between the two verticals leads to a better understanding of speech characteristics and can reduce the gap between human and machine performance. [Figure 4.4](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_004) presents the relation between speech production and perception.

![Figure 4.4: Speech production and perception system in humans.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_004.png)

Figure 4.4: Speech production and perception system in humans.

### 4.3.2 Automatic speech recognition

Speech is the most natural and convenient mode of communication between human. Using this medium as the communication between human and machine has now become the most desired mode. This interaction requires the conversion of the spoken utterance of human into machine-understandable form. Automatic speech recognition is the method for doing this task. ASR provides the conversion of spoken utterances into text with the use of computer algorithm. Several potential applications such as dictation system, command and control instructions, and information retrieval use this technology. These systems range from isolated speech recognition to recognition of highly conversational speech signals. The ASR systems can also be categorized based on the vocabulary size ranging from small (up to 1000 words) to extra-large (more than a hundred thousand words) ([Whittaker and Woodland, 2001](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_028)).

[Figure 4.5](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_005) presents the general architecture of a standard ASR system. The ASR system is the integration of three major components: the acoustic model, the lexical model, and the language model. ASR systems work in two phases starting with the parameterization of input utterances followed by training and testing of systems using those features. At the core of the system, the parameters representing various sound units of the language are used to develop a statistical model. During the first phase, that is, the training process creates models for speech acoustics (recorded sample of a large number of users are required), language statistics (use of text data for grammar knowledge and n-gram models), and recognition dictionary (lexicon of recognizable tokens and their phonetic transcription with several variations). The acoustic model created here can be context-dependent (allophone, triphone, etc.) as well as context-independent (monophones) models that capture speech, speaker, and channel variability.

![Figure 4.5: The general architecture of the ASR system.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_005.png)

Figure 4.5: The general architecture of the ASR system.

During the training, features in terms of vectors are extracted for the building of acoustic models. These speech features can be any of the linear prediction coefficients, Mel frequency cepstral coefficients (MFCC), perceptual linear prediction, gammatone frequency cepstral coefficient, and basilar-membrane frequency-band cepstral coefficient or the hybrid features (MF-PLP) retrieved as the combination of multiple feature sets ([Dua et al., 2018](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_010)). The feature extraction process helps in dimensionality reduction as well as probabilistic modeling. Recognition dictionary creation is a step toward lexical modeling. It assigns each orthographic token of the dictionary to the spoken utterance (can be phoneme, syllable, or word). Language modeling helps in the selection of the most appropriate recognition hypothesis that is obtained during acoustic modeling. The language model also helps in modeling the syntax, semantics, and structure of the target language. The goodness of the language model is based on the size and variety of text data used for the empirical estimate.

For the acoustic model creation, today’s ASR uses techniques like hidden Markov model (HMM) ([Young et al., 2008](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_029)), dynamic programming ([Jing et al., 2010](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_017)), support vector machine (SVM) ([Solera-Urena et al., 2007](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_024)), and artificial neural networks ([Mohamed et al., 2011](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_020)). Recently, the model creation approach has been extended to use deep neural network ([Upadhyaya et al., 2019](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_025)) and dynamic belief networks. The system depicted in [Figure 4.5](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_005) is based upon the stochastic HMM-based approach. These speech decoders use a method that implements token passing and is created using the Viterbi algorithm ([Young et al., 2008](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_029)). They can generate word/phoneme N-best lists representing the recognition hypotheses which are rescored using the language model and selects the one with the best score. Availability of easily adaptable, open-source toolkits, such as Julius, Sphinx, HTK, and Kaldi, has helped the developers to produce ASR applications in any target language.

**ASR performance:** The most common measure to evaluate any ASR system performance is the word error rate (WER%). This parameter is evaluated by comparing the transcription output with a reference transcript. This error can be further used to evaluate three categories of errors.

*   Insertion (I): Unit present in the output transcript but does not exist in the input sample.
    
*   Deletion (D): Unit missing in the transcript output wherein it exists in the input sample.
    
*   Substitution (S): A word/unit confused and hence replaced with another unit in the output transcript.
    

With these thee error categories, the WER% is computed as WER% = (I + D + S)/*N*, where *N* is the number of reference words. Apart from that, in a few languages, the performance is computed in terms of the character error rate and utterance error rate.

### 4.3.3 Machine translation

With the growth in the economy showing a borderless phenomenon across the globe, the demand for language translation from one language to the other has surged. MT uses computer software to convert text or speech of one language to another while preserving the meaning of the source language. It can be comprehended as a substitution of a word in one language with the word in another. Soon after the advent of ENIAC computer in the year 1947, research was initiated for using computers as a device for translating natural languages. These efforts ranged from developing a simple translation dictionary to a full-fledged MT system ([Dorr et al., 1999](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_009); [Doddington, 2002](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_008)). But, for long no success was achieved in this area, and in the year 1966 it was declared that semantic barrier between the languages could not be resolved by machine and hence the goal of MT cannot be achieved. In around 1988, *IBM candid system* was launched in the market which used to provide translation for around 60 Russian sentences ([Antony, 2013](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_001)). After this success, the research continued using advanced methods and new techniques and somewhere around 1990s statistical machine translation (SMT) technology came into existence. This development changed the way the world used to see the language barrier ([Bouillon et al., 2017](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_006)). The success of SMT grabbed the attention of the IT sector and application based on MT were developed using the complex hybrid methods ([Nair et al., 2016](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_021)).

MT systems can be created for one language pair called the bilingual system that can be bidirectional too, or it can be created for multiple language pair also. The system capable of handling translation between multiple sources and target languages is called a multilingual system. Creation of a bidirectional multilingual system is difficult, and so they are mainly unidirectional. One of the major challenges in creation of MT system is the demand for large corpora created parallelly for the two languages. Another challenge in the development of automatic translation system is related to the ambiguities among the languages. Any of the syntax, semantics, lexicon, or contextual ambiguity can make the process difficult. To handle these linguistic challenges, the text is kept limited to few domains so that lexicon and grammar become more restricted. If further extensions to the domain are required in future, the system has to be adapted to new domain and its lexicon. However, till date none of the state-of-the-art MT systems exist that can be used with high confidence in real-word scenario ([Nair et al., 2016](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_021)). The use of neural machine translation with long–short-term memory units has performed remarkably toward meeting the real-time goal. The initiative by Google to develop *Google Translate* for translation over 100 languages is noteworthy, but again its performance in the open domain needs attention. To obtain a quality result from MT system, postediting efforts are applied, which can be computer-aided support for grammar and spell checker or can be a human-assisted translation, that is, manual intervention. Success in the field of automatic translation can help remove the language barrier and can make available the rich literature of one language in other languages of the world.

The translation from one language to the another can follow a direct conversion architecture or the interlingual architecture. In the former case, the syntactic conversion between one language and the other language is done based on the specified rule, whereas the later focusses on semantic transfer. In the case of interlingua architecture, the input data is first represented in text that is independent of the source language. [Figure 4.6](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_008) presents the pyramid of MT approaches. The text of the second language, that is, the target language is generated using this language-independent intermediate data making it easier for translating into several languages. In between the two architectures exist a technique that works on transfer-based architecture. Here, the source language is analyzed syntactically to obtain intermediate representation, and then rules are used to map the representation to the target language. Another approach used for MT is to follow the statistical approach for translation. The SMT can be word-based, phrase-based, or syntax-based. They do not require an explicit rule description for the conversion process. Apart from these knowledge-based MT, empirical MT ([Antony, 2013](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_001)) approaches are also in use in the translation system. Hybrid MT approach, which combines the multiple approaches in building a single system has been widely used recently. These approaches perform satisfactorily as they exploit the strength of individual approaches to complete the task.

![Figure 4.6: Machine translation pyramid.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_006.png)

Figure 4.6: Machine translation pyramid.

#### 4.3.3.1 MT performance

Performance evaluation of MT can be done manually as well as by using metric defined for machine-based evaluation. The manual evaluation requires evaluators with the knowledge of both the source and the target languages. Fluency and adequacy are the two major parameters used in the manual evaluation. Apart from this, BLEU ([Papineni et al., 2002](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_022)) and METEOR ([Banerjee and Lavie, 2005](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_003)) are other methods for automatic evaluation. Each of these methods is based upon n-grams (unigram, bigram, trigram) in the corpus. Different weight assignment approaches are used by these scoring techniques to obtain precision and recall values.

### 4.3.4 Text-to-speech synthesis

To generate the natural resonant from the random text is the end objective of TTS. Speech synthesis refers to the artificial production of the human voice. Different categories of speech synthesizers exist with diverse technological variations due to software and hardware. The excellence of any TTS system is based on two prime aspects – naturalness and intelligibility. Naturalness is the resemblance to natural human speech; on the other side, intelligibility is the ease of understanding by the listener. The TTS system always makes efforts to maximize both aspects ([Raitio et al., 2010](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_023)).

TTS conversion is done in two phases. During the first phase, a phonetic transliteration of the input text is obtained and the second phase uses the statistics obtained in the first phase to produce voice output. The front-end of the TTS system is responsible for the preprocessing of text and also the grapheme to phoneme (g2p) conversion. During this process, written words are produced for the digits, numerals, and abbreviations, if any, present in the text. At the time of g2p conversion, each phonetic representation is mapped into prosodic units, that is, the sentence, clauses, or the phrases. At the back end, the output produced is utilized to obtain the speech signal. The most suitable application area of a speech synthesizer is the creation of screen readers for visually impaired and blind users. Also, it can be utilized for the development of communicational aids for a speech-impaired person. TTS synthesizers can be very helpful for dyslexic people.

Several methods exist for speech synthesis, and they can be categorized as articulatory synthesis, formant synthesis, or concatenative synthesis. First, of these, the articulatory speech synthesis is based on the biomechanical models, which are responsibly initiating the speech signal in human being. The articulatory synthesizers imitate the influence of the articulators (tongue, lips, glottis, and VT). This reproduction is three-dimensional differential equation which is time-dependent and computes the artificial speech output. The major requirement of such type of models is the availability of enormously high computational resources. Besides this, the output obtained by these models does not produce fluent speech and lack naturality ([Beutnagel et al., 1999](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_004)). Formant speech synthesis comprises the source-filter model, based on the hypothesis that the source (glottal) and filter can be treated independently ([Jilka et al., 2003](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_016)). The action of the filter is based on the bandwidth and formant frequencies. The advantage of the formant synthesis method is that it requires moderate computational resources for generating extremely comprehensible speech, though it does not produce entirely natural speech. The last approach is that the concatenative speech synthesis uses minimal sized speech units, named phonemes. The phonemes are recorded in a noise-free environment to develop a speech database. These recorded phonemes can be stored either as a waveform or in an encoded signal obtained with a suitable speech-encoding algorithm. The speech produced with this method uses recorded elementary units of actual sounds; therefore, this method has the highest potential for generating natural speech ([Klatt, 1987](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_019)).

HMM is one of the most common techniques to simulate the observed behavior of any process. This capability of HMM is also utilized for modeling human speech behavior in speech technology. The model works in two passes. In the first pass, called the training phase, the HMM learns the acoustic-prosodic aspect of the speech. Subsequently, in the second phase, that is, the speech generation phase, HMMs extract most likely parameters of the text to be synthesized. HMMs can be trained in two different ways; first, with a long speech corpus of around 2–4 h speech from a single speaker; second, an adaptive training approach that requires speech samples from multiple speakers and adapt it to a specific speaker’s voice characteristics. This approach requires a small corpus for synthesizing speech. Whatever be the approach followed, in the training phase HMM uses the speech corpus, its phonetic transcription extended to cover the context dependency, and marked phoneme boundaries. The speech features such as MFCC and pitch along with their derivatives are extracted from the waveform. With the help of these parameters, HMMs learn the spectral and excitation parameters. [Figure 4.7](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_007) presents the HMM-based training of the speech synthesizers.

![Figure 4.7: HMM-based speech synthesizer training module.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_007.png)

Figure 4.7: HMM-based speech synthesizer training module.

Once the training is over, the speech generation process starts. This process requires the phonetic transcription and context-dependent labelling of the text to be read. HMMs create the spectral and excitation parameters from the phone duration extracted from the state duration density function. Synthesizes speech is obtained using these parameters. To obtain a good quality speech, mixed excitation model is applied. Gnuspeech, free TTS, and Festival Speech Synthesis system are a few tools that can be used for building a TTS system.

## 4.4 Performance evaluation

A TTS system can be evaluated in several aspects. Based on the purpose of building the system, the evaluation metric is selected. The performance of the TTS system can be measured to evaluate the naturalness using mean opinion score (MOS) associated with the quality of speech. The second parameter for evaluation is the intelligibility of the system that is measured in terms of MOS or WER. Apart from these, accuracies and comprehensibility are other measures for evaluation of a TTS system.

## 4.5 Addressing language barriers in healthcare

The language barrier is considered as one of the most dominant obstructions in globalization. The necessity to resolve the barrier is much more needed in the medical domain. Migration of the Indian population to the other part of the world is dominant and is still increasing day by day. Not all moving out of the country is literate and can communicate only in their mother tongue. On the other hand, most of the countries have their own language or they use English as one of the media of communication. India also uses English as one of the official languages, but the proportion of population who are not well educated are not able to communicate in English. To overcome this language barrier, a speech translation is needed between Indian languages and English. Several efforts ([Vemula et al., 2010](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_026)) have been made in this direction but most of them are in the travel domain. Medical facilities being one of the crucial and most needed facility demands attention of the research community. Research to overcome the language barrier in healthcare services has started throughout the world ([Ettelaie et al., 2005](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_012); [Ehsani et al., 2006](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_011)). An S2S system in the medical domain will be helpful to Indian migrants in the Middle East countries and also to other developed countries of the world where people move to seek the best possible healthcare services for critical diseases.

Speech technology-based systems are highly data-driven systems, and their success is mainly dependent on the corpus available in the concerned languages and domain database ([Kathol et al., 2005](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_ref_018)). This section presents an S2S translation system that works for Hindi to English translation and vice versa. Hindi being an underresourced language the creation of such a system is nontrivial, majorly due to unavailability of data and that too in the medical domain. Analysis of work done in the medical domain shows that applications in the medical/healthcare domain that use speech technologies can be placed in different categories:

*   Dictation systems: Mainly used by doctors to provide notes to nurses/receptionists.
    
*   Interactive systems: These voice-enabled systems are used to handle the interaction between patients and caretakers, patient and doctor, or patient and receptionists for making an appointment.
    
*   Command and control systems: Generally used for controlling medical equipment.
    
*   Language interpretation systems: These systems are mainly used to reduce the language barrier by providing translation services to the communication partners of different languages.
    

All the work done in this domain marks that the medical domain utilizes a large corpus of words from the clinical/medicinal domain such as radiology, dental science, and pediatrics. A specialized corpus for development of S2S in the medical domain has to be created. We define the pipeline architecture used for S2S system to provide translation from Hindi to English and vice versa. The system discussed here works for speech translation between the patient’s language and that of the medical practitioners. The system provides end-to-end translation but has the facility at each intermediate level for evaluation and correction by human interpreters. This feature helps in identifying the error patterns and directs for further improvements. With time, when the errors are rectified, we look forward to removing the provision for human interventions. This can only be achieved when all the three models: ASR, MT, and TTS are tuned to perform perfectly.

### 4.5.1 Medical corpus creation

In the first phase of the system creation, we focus on the development of system that deals with phrasal communication in medical domain and interactions in question–answer format. In the initial phase, we have avoided the fully conversational speech due to disfluencies, fillers, and other factors that make it difficult. The question–answer corpus mainly consists of queries that are raised by patients to be answered by medical practitioners and also questions based on history and symptoms queried by doctors and replied by patients. [Table 4.2](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_tab_002) shows the statistics of the corpus created for this purpose.

Table 4.2:Corpus specifications for the speech translation system.

 
| Corpus specifications  |
| --- |
| **Speakers statistics** |
| --- |
| Male: 58 speakers || Age >18 years and <65 years |
| Female: 42 speakers || Age >18 years and <60 years |
| **Sentence style** |
| Question answer format: 500 sentences || Hindi: मुझे कौन सी दवाईयाँ लेनी हैं डॉक्टर?  
English: Which medicines do I need to take doctor? |
| Phrasal sentences || Hindi: मुझे सांस लेने में तकलीफ हो रही है|  
English: I have difficulty in breathing. |
| **Sentence average length** |
| Question–answer format: 8 words/sentence || Phrasal sentences: 5 words/phrase |
| **Recording specifications** |
| Tool: PRAAT, Ver:3.9 || Recording parameters: 16 bit, 16 KHz, Mono |

One peculiar feature of the corpus is related to medical terminologies. The frequency of medical terms is less in the corpora as we have used doctor–patient interaction pattern and it is more likely that the doctor–patient conversation will be in general language terms instead of excessive use of medical terminologies.

### 4.5.2 Experimental setup

The pipeline architecture of the system starts with the ASR module that receives input for training the system. The ASR module of this system is built using Sphinx3, an HMM-based speech recognizer. The SphinxTrain module is provided with the phone list, mapping dictionary, filler dictionary, phonetic transcript of the wave files, and speech recording files. The trainer uses these files to learn the parameters and the decoder of the Sphinx that produces the most appropriate sequence of words for the test input. The bilingual MT module is built using NLTK toolkit. The module is implemented using rule-based translation for English to Hindi and Example-based translation for Hindi to English. The challenging TTS module has been built using Festival and Festvox framework. To produce a human-like speech, the HMM-based TTS module receives the input text produced by the MT, and the phonetic representation is further marked into prosodic units. WaveSurfer is used at this level for manual alignment of labels and phones. [Figure 4.8](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_008) represents the manual labelling performed on English sentence using WaveSurfer. The prosodic unit is used to produce natural speech for the input text. The overall architecture demands a list of phone sets for both the languages used in the system.

![Figure 4.8: Manual segmentation and labelling using WaveSurfer showing power plot, formants, and pitch.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_008.jpg)

Figure 4.8: Manual segmentation and labelling using WaveSurfer showing power plot, formants, and pitch.

### 4.5.3 Performance evaluation

Each component of the translation system is evaluated separately. Since the overall performance of the system is dependent on the performance by each unit of the pipelined components, we have evaluated the component performance individually. This evaluation also helps in identifying the component, which requires more attention to further improvements. The ASR component performance is evaluated in terms of WER%, which takes into account the count of inserted, deleted, and substituted words. The performance of the MT component reliability is judged using METEOR, a method that is highly correlated with human evaluation. It takes into account the precision and recall, assigning more weights to recall. TTS component was evaluated using a perception test. Ten listeners were identified, and the speech synthesizer for the input samples was played for them. They were asked to judge the sample on a three-point measuring scale that ranged from unclear speech, difficult to comprehend, and understandable speech.

A randomly selected 30 question–answer-based samples and 50 phrasal sentences were used for testing the data for both the languages. The evaluation was done in six iterations with each iteration of same sample size and sample selection were done on random basis. [Figure 4.9](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_009) presents the performance of each of the ASR, MT, and TTS component in all the six iterations. The average WER of the ASR module turned out to be 0.6743, that is, the average accuracy of the ASR system is 67.43% for Hindi speech recognition and for English it was obtained as 72%. This low performance may be attributed to ambient noise or also to the device microphone quality as the recordings were done using mobile phones.

![Figure 4.9: Performance of ASR, MT, and TTS subsystems in different iterations.](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-004_fig_009.png)

Figure 4.9: Performance of ASR, MT, and TTS subsystems in different iterations.

The average performance score of machine translation system turned out to be 0.6621; 66.21% correct translation for Hindi to English and 64.32% for English to Hindi translation. The representation in [Figure 4.9](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_fig_009) corresponds to Hindi to English conversion. [Table 4.3](#bodymatter-004.xhtml#b_9783110648195-004_tab_003) shows the user perception for a few samples of Hindi speech synthesizer.

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sample 1 || 2 || 1 || 2 || 2 || 1 || 2 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sample 2 || 3 || 2 || 2 || 3 || 3 || 2 || 2 || 2 || 3 || 2 |
| Sample 3 || 2 || 2 || 3 || 3 || 2 || 2 || 1 || 2 || 2 || 3 |
| Sample 4 || 3 || 2 || 2 || 2 || 3 || 3 || 2 || 2 || 2 || 2 |

The average performance of the Hindi speech synthesizer was rated as 2.03 on a scale of 3 and for English, the performance was evaluated as 2.12 on the same scale.

## 4.6 Conclusion

In this chapter, we discussed the language barrier in healthcare services. Importance and necessity of speech technology for removing the language barrier has been presented here. We identify the challenges associated with the medical domain in reference to technology building for automatic communication. A brief review of S2S systems existent in different domains has been covered here. The analysis of the review helped to find a way to overcome the language barrier with the help of speech technology and lead to the development of the S2S system in healthcare. Proceeding further we discuss here the S2S translation system for Hindi to English and vice versa in healthcare services. Each component of the system is separately discussed and a pipeline architecture of ASR-MT-TTS is described to reduce the language barrier. The performance of the subcomponents is analyzed separately. It shows that our system performed fairly well and further improvements can be obtained by working on the shortcomings of each component. The ASR can be made more noise-robust, and MT performance can be enhanced by utilizing several hypotheses for the input text. It is evident that in future the use of speech technology will become essential to break the language barrier, especially in healthcare sector.

## 4.7 Future scope

In future the primary aim is to enhance the accuracy of the existing system. Speech-enabled services are data-centric; hence, we need to increase the size of speech corpus for increasing the accuracy percentage. Apart from this, integration of noise removal algorithm in the existing system will also help to improve the system performance. India is a country of several languages. Extending the voice-enabled services to the people of other languages will ease their way to global healthcare services. We intend to develop multilingual speech translation system in healthcare sector that would cover more Indian languages along with languages of other countries where better healthcare services are available. The corpus needs to cover variations of accents due to the social and economic background of speakers to increase system robustness.

Nowadays, use of mobile phones is expanding rapidly and has covered most of rural India also. Extending the system for deployment on mobile phones will provide a worthwhile service in the healthcare sector for rural areas.

# 5 Recent advancement of machine learning and deep learning in the field of healthcare system

Yogesh Kumar Chandigarh Engineering College, Landran, Mohali Manish Mahajan Chandigarh Engineering College, Landran, Mohali 

#### Abstract

The healthcare sector has long been adapted primarily and significantly from scientific advances. Nowadays, machine learning (ML, a subset of artificial intelligence) plays a vital role in numerous health-related domains, including the expansion of novel medical measures, managing patient information and records, and treatment of chronic ailments. ML in medicine has recently made headlines. Google has developed an ML technique to help recognize cancerous tumors on mammograms. Stanford uses a deep learning method to classify skin cancer diseases. Still, ML advances itself to developments better than other terminologies. Algorithms can deliver instant advantage to disciplines with procedures that are reproducible or consistent. Also, those with huge number of medical image datasets, such as radiology, pathology, and cardiology, are robust aspirants. ML can be qualified to look at images, classify irregularities, and opinion to parts that require attention, thus improving the correctness of all these developments. In future, ML will provide benefits to the family physician at home. ML can also offer an objective opinion to improve productivity, consistency, and accurateness. Healthcare needs to interchange from intelligence of ML as an innovative perception to sight it as a real-world tool that can be organized nowadays. We must take an incremental approach if ML has to play a role in healthcare system. ML has boundless impression in the area of healthcare such as drug discovery applications, robotic surgery, predicting diabetics, liver abnormality, and also in personalized healthcare. The main aim of the chapter is to study the advancement of ML in recent healthcare applications such as automatic treatment or recommendation for different diseases, automatic robotic surgery, drug discovery and development, and other latest domains of the healthcare system. The chapter also comprises the analysis of different ML techniques used in healthcare. Another objective of the chapter provides a systematic procedure to use ML techniques on healthcare domains. The hybrid ML methods can also be used to detect different types of diseases. Using ML algorithms, the efficient system that identifies multicancer diseases can be developed at the same time. The chapter also comprises the analysis based on ML methods and deep learning methods in healthcare system.

Keywords:  Machine learning,  Deep learning,  Cancer disease,  Robotic surgery,  Classification,  

## 5.1 Introduction

Industrial organizations are using large amount of data that is recognized using machine learning (ML). By collecting perceptions from these data, a more efficient work can be done by organization and can get better results than their competitors ([Amin et al., 2013](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_003)). In various domains, ML algorithms have been used in innovative predictive models ([Anand et al., 2015](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_004)). Even deep learning techniques are used in various day-to-day activities such as advertisement, Google Search, and YouTube. The use of ML and deep learning techniques in the field of multidisciplinary and healthcare informatics has become the same in terms of data handling challenges and technological advertisements ([LeCun et al., 2015](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_029); [Russakovsky, 2015](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_038)). The scientific field such as health or medical informatics deal with storing, retrieving, and optimal use of medical data as well as information along with a knowledge provided for problem solving and decision making. Over the last few decades, an immense development has been achieved in health technology such as advances in information gathering, communication, research, and treatments. By medical information digitization, there is an increase in patient’s data that can be related to advanced equipment-generated medical results and data provided by patients ([Hirschberg et al., 2015](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_025)). This information revolution is a common outcome, where people face disheartened tasks of interpreting and understanding data. Handling or managing these large amount of data is not possible by simply using manual techniques and tools, and is also a very slow and tedious process. So, to understand the data there is a need of data-driven approaches from computational sciences or data science and data analytics. Critical health information can be extracted, and the medical data can be analyzed using these approaches that can help doctors and patients in making better decisions ([Hinton et al., 2012](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_022)).

Currently for analyzing medical data, a large investment is done in data science by academia and industry. In the coming years, an important role will be played by data science due to rapid growth in the amount of generated data in medicine. Before the deployment of artificial intelligence (AI) systems in healthcare applications, there is a need to train it through clinical activities such as diagnosis, treatment, and screening assessments that generate data ([Kotsiantis, 2007](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_028)). This is done to learn AI by similar groups of subjects and associations between outcomes and features of interest. There are two major categories of AI devices.

First one with ML technique: This will analyze structured data such as genetic, eligible provider (EP) , and imaging data and are used in medical applications that attempt to find the probability of disease outcomes ([Brazdil et al., 2003](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_011)).

Other with natural language processing (NLP): This will extract information from medical journals and clinical notes like unstructured data that supplements and enriches the structured medical data ([Cheng et al., 2007](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_012)). The main target of NLP procedures is to turn texts into machine-readable structured data that is further analyzed using ML techniques.

In this chapter, Section 5.1 provides a brief introduction about the use of ML and deep learning in healthcare; Section 5.2 contains the classification of ML methods that consist of four types. Section 5.3 gives a systematic procedure of using ML techniques in healthcare domains. Section 5.4 gives applications of ML in healthcare that contains work done by various researchers in different areas of healthcare along with the comparative study. Deep learning solutions in healthcare and other industries along with the comparative study are given in Section 5.5, and Section 5.6 contains current and future applications of deep learning in healthcare and medical domain.

## 5.2 Classification of ML methods

There are large numbers of ML algorithms that are classified into four broad categories on the basis of learning nature ([Mishra, 2018](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_033)). The selection process of ML algorithm consists of features that match the learned data with the existing approach biases. [Figure 5.1](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_fig_001) shows the types of ML.

![Figure 5.1: Types of machine learning process (Mishra, 2018).](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-005_fig_001.png)

Figure 5.1: Types of machine learning process ([Mishra, 2018](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_033)).

### 5.2.1 Supervised learning

Another name given to supervised learning is the predictive model, which is used for tasks such as use of other values in the dataset including prediction of one value ([Anil Mathur, 2007](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_005)). The process of predictive model training is identified as supervised learning due to a clear instruction given to predictive models about what and how they need to learn. In existence of uncertainty, the supervised ML is able to predict the evidence in existence. [Figure 5.2](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_fig_002) shows the diagram of speech learning. As in supervised learning algorithms, an output is taken from an input dataset and known data response. Some of the supervised ML algorithms are as follows:

![Figure 5.2: Supervised learning (Anil Mathur, 2007).](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-005_fig_002.png)

Figure 5.2: Supervised learning ([Anil Mathur, 2007](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_005)).

*   **Support vector machine (SVM):** SVM is considered as the most recent type of supervised ML technique that is closely related to classical multilayer perceptron neural networks (NNs) ([Yang et al., 2018](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_045)). It works on the margin of objects from hyperplane that is drawn for separating two or more than two objects or data classes ([Zhang et al., 2012](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_046)). The expected generalization errors will be reduced by reducing the margin or creating a large distance between the instances and hyperplane separating it.
    
*   **Naïve Bayes (NB):** During the stage of classification and training, there is a need of very less storage space in NB supervised ML method ([Zhu et al., 2009](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_047)). That means to store the prior and conditional probabilities, there is a need of very less storage space that results in very less storing memory for training phases but the execution space should be equal to the space of training part.
    
*   **NN:** The NN supervised ML methods or techniques are able to perform various tasks of regression and classification but only a single task is performed by an individual network ([Hiregoudar et al., 2014](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_024)). Therefore, in most of the cases there is only single output variable in the network but there can be a number of output units while working on cases having many state classification problems.
    
*   **Decision tree (DT):** These trees classify instances by simply sorting them on the basis of values of feature. A feature is represented as an instance to be classified by each node in a DT and an assumed value by each node is represented by each branch in it ([Gillies et al., 2012](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_016)). The classification of instances starts from the root node, and then according to the values of feature, they are sorted out.
    
*   **Bayesian network (BN):** It is a graphical model for relationships of probability among various sets of features or any other variable. It is considered as a very famous representative of structured ML algorithms ([Shin et al., 2010](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_040)). As compared to NN or DT supervised ML methods, a most important or different feature of BN is that it considers the possibility of taking prior information about any problem.
    

### 5.2.2 Unsupervised learning

Its another name is a descriptive model that is used for tasks that will benefit from understanding summarized data in interesting and new ways. With predictive models, a target of interest is predictive; on the other hand, any feature is a descriptive model that will be more important than any other single feature. Unsupervised learning is the process used for training a descriptive model due to no involvement of learning goal for distinguishing the data and gets few structures ([Kaelbling, 1996](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_026)). It is mainly used on transactional data. Some of the unsupervised ML algorithms are as follows:

*   ***k*\-Means clustering:** This is a type of unsupervised ML technique that creates groups automatically on initiation of it. Then the items having same characteristics come under the same cluster ([Li, 2016](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_030)). As it creates *k* distinct clusters according to the input data given to it, this algorithm is known as *k*\-means. In any particular cluster, its mean values are the center of that cluster.
    
*   Fuzzy *c*\-means
    
*   Hidden Markov model
    

They are mainly used to segment text topics and identify data outliers.

**Semisupervised learning:** It can be used in place of supervised learning. The only difference is to train both labeled and unlabeled data used in semisupervised learning. This type of learning is mostly used in prediction, regression, and classification. And in cases of high labeling cost for a fully labeled training process, a semisupervised learning gives beneficial results ([Thompson, 2007](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_042)). One of the examples in using semisupervised ML algorithm is in recognizing the face of persons on a web.

**Reinforcement learning (RL):** In this case, a machine is trained to have same decisions in business requirement with the main motive to have maximum efficiency. In it there is less participation of human expertise that results in saving of time. It also helps in discovering the trial and error through algorithms that help in having best rewards. It is most often used in gaming, navigation, and robotics or for decision making. Some of the examples of RL ML algorithms are:

*   **Multitask learning:** The main motive of multitask learning technique of RL is to help learners so that it can perform in a better way. The procedure of solving any problem is remembered by multitask learning algorithms that help to reach a particular conclusion. After that these steps are used by algorithms that help in finding the similar solution as found while working earlier on the same task or problem. This help is also known as an inductive transfer mechanism in which learners share their experience with each other that helps them all to learn concurrently in a faster way rather than individually ([Darcy et al., 2016](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_014)).
    
*   **Ensemble learning:** When a number of individual learners are combined, it is known as ensemble learning. In this case, an individual learner can be a DT, NB, NN, and so on. It has been observed that a better result can be achieved by a collection of learners rather than individual learners ([Murff et al., 2011](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_036)).
    
*   Q-learning
    
*   R-learning
    
*   TD learning
    

## 5.3 Systematic procedure to use ML techniques on healthcare domains

Due to storage and generation of large amounts of data, using ML is getting increased in enterprise predictions. In order to have high accuracy and value to enterprise datasets, an effective framework, applications and algorithms, is provided by ML. On the basis of large-scale datasets predictive value, an optimized decision is to find out using ML methodologies. The use of it is effective in handling predictive tasks such as defining behavior that has maximum tendency for driving the preferred outcomes.

### 5.3.1 Steps to apply ML to data

Below are some of the steps for applying ML task:

*   Data collection
    
*   Exploring and preparing the data
    
*   Training a model on the data
    
*   Evaluating performance of model
    
*   Improving performance of model
    
*   **Data collection:** This is the first block of [Figure 5.3](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_fig_003). For analysis purpose, data needs to be gathered in an electronic format irrespective of whether it is written on paper, stored in SQL database, and recorded in spreadsheets and text files. According to this gathered data, an algorithm generates an actionable information.
    

![Figure 5.3: Machine learning process (Fathurachman et al., 2014).](graphic/converted/b_9783110648195-005_fig_003.png)

Figure 5.3: Machine learning process ([Fathurachman et al., 2014](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_015)).

*   **Exploring and preparing the data:** On the basis of used data quality, an ML project quality is measured. In this step of ML process, there is more involvement of humans. In ML, 80% of effort is dedicated to data, and data exploration is the time spent on learning about data.
    
*   **Training a model on the data:** The best algorithm is chosen by ML and then data is represented in the form of model by the chosen algorithm.
    
*   **Evaluating model performance:** The estimation performance of algorithm learned from its past experience is very important. Each ML model is able to have a biased solution for each problem related to learning. On the basis of used model test, dataset can be used to evaluate the accuracy of the model.
    
*   **Improve model performance:** In order to have a better performance, the performance of each model is enhanced using advanced strategies. There is a need to change the model according to the requirements or to have required results.
    

After completing these steps, a proposed task is done by deploying a model that is able to achieve acceptable performance. For financial data projection, score data prediction, medical application data, or to automate a task, an ML model is applied. The deployed model failures and successes give the data to train the model for next generation.

## 5.4 Applications of ML in healthcare

There is a very fast growth in population that makes it challenging to analyze and record a large amount of patient’s data. The healthcare systems become more robust and dynamic with the help of ML that automates the processing and find out data from large database. Medical science and computer science are two types of domains that come in single thread using ML in healthcare. The use of ML techniques helps in advancement of medical science and analyzing the complex medical data for further analysis.

### 5.4.1 ML in heart disease

Till 2012, an experienced physician judgment was the only way to diagnose coronary heart disease. At that time, current topic was to use computer-intelligent algorithms for coronary heart disease diagnosis in ML. Then coronary heart disease detection was done by [Zhang et al. (2012)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_046), using statistical learning theory-based SVM. In their work, the test data is classified using classifiers with different kernels along with feature extraction and data preprocessing of original data. They have concluded that radial basis function (RBF) accuracy is highest than all other classifiers. For RBF, an optimization parameter grid search method was used for selecting a best parameter of kernel function and penalty factor. Various researchers have achieved good accuracy in diagnosis of heart disease using artificial neural network (ANN) technique. But still there are some drawbacks of ANN technique such as tuning of number of parameters, activation function must be differentiable, long training process, and obtained solution gets stuck in local minima. Due to all these disadvantages, [Fathurachman et al. (2014)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_015) have used an extreme learning machine (ELM). The ELM is considered as better generalization model learning algorithm that is faster than DT, SVM, and ANN and also has simple tuning. The results obtained using ELM model shows that it helps in diagnosing heart disease with an accuracy of 83%. Due to involvement of large amount of data, large noise interference, and high data dimension, diagnosis of heart disease is a difficult task that creates a need of using data-driven approaches. So, for diagnosis of heart diseases, [Yang et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_045) have presented an SVM-based two-dimension–reduction methodologies. For the purpose of diagnosis, more relevant features are achieved using SVM-recursive feature elimination method than for the principle component analysis SVM. The proposed approaches are evaluated, which shows that PCA-SVM is able to achieve 88.24% of classification accuracy via RBF kernel using only size principal components.

### 5.4.2 Using ML to predict diabetes

As mentioned in the last section, there is rapid advancement in technology and an increase in data that makes a healthcare domain as very important field for researchers. Big data analytics approach has started using in dealing with this large amount of healthcare data. [Mir et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_032) motive is to propose a WEKA tool using a classifier model that is able to predict the diabetes disease. For analysis purpose, they have used random forest, SVM, simple CART, and NB algorithm. On the basis of efficient results achieved by evaluating each algorithm on the dataset in prediction of diabetes diseases, a best algorithm is find out by them. From performance, it has been observed that in prediction of diabetic disease SVM gives maximum accuracy. As like other diseases, early prediction of diabetes can save human life. Due to this various diabetes-related risk factors are explored by [Md. Faruque et al. (2019)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_031), using ML techniques as it was able to give efficient results in terms of knowledge extraction. Prediction models are constructed for extracting knowledge from collected diagnostic medical datasets of diabetic patients. This helps in predicting diabetic patients. In their work to predict diabetic mellitus, they have mainly used adult population data such as *k*\-nearest neighbor (*k*\-NN), NB, C4.5 DT, and SVM ML algorithms. On all these four algorithms, an experiment was conducted that shows C4.5 DT with highest accuracy.

Then again in 2019, [Alic et al. (2019)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_002) have used ML for predicting the future development of type-2 diabetes. For analysis purpose, a San Antonio Heart study data was used and ten features along with SVM were used to build the prediction model. A 10-fold cross-validation was used for training model because in terms of class labels a dataset was unbalanced. In average of 100 iterations, an 81.1% recall rate and an 84.1% of accuracy are achieved that help in identification of the population who was at high risk of developing type-2 diabetes.

### 5.4.3 ML in prediction of liver abnormalities

The automation is used for prediction of liver abnormalities using ultrasound images. To do it, [Hamid et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_018) have used ML model for generating normal and abnormal labels and also detect when its prediction is likely to be incorrect. If the proposed model is not able to predict the abnormalities, then it will not generate the label of a test example. The currently used ML models still generate a label for a given example even in the cases of low prediction confidence. Because of it for learning a solver-based novel stochastic gradient decent was proposed by Kanza and then practical, state-of-the-art method was proposed using it. This method was proposed for liver disease classification and then its testing was done using dataset of 100 patients from Pakistan, Multan, and MINAR. To avoid the drawbacks of biopsy, noninvasive ML approaches are used. [Hashem et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_020) have used a number of ML techniques (genetic algorithm, multilinear regression, particle swarm optimization, and DT) for predicting advanced fibrosis. In this they have combined the clinical information and serum biomarkers for developing a classification model. There were around 40,000 patients who were divided into two sets of prospective cohorts with chronic hepatitis C. To advanced fibrosis age, aspartate transaminase, albumin, and platelet count were found to be significant that has been found by evaluating the proposed model with receiver operating characteristics. The AUROC and HCC achieved while predicting advanced fibrosis in patient is between 0.73 and 0.76, and accuracies achieved using ML algorithms were 66.3% and 84.4%. [Vats et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_044) have worked on *k*\-means, affinity propagation, and density-based spatial clustering of applications with noise (DBSCAN) ML techniques due to its popularity and good results achieved in the field of data science. It is further compared in terms of computational complexity and prediction accuracy of liver disease prediction in patients. The liver disease-related healthcare dataset was used, and the performance measurement of the above-mentioned three ML algorithms was compared using the Silhouette coefficient. According to this measurement, a *k*\-means is an optimal method in predicting liver diseases.

### 5.4.4 Automatic robotic surgery using ML

To evaluate disease progression and surgical planning, measuring tissue mechanical properties and localizing tumors have been proved to be useful. Then in artificial tissues, a stiff inclusion mechanical localization was enabled by supervised ML algorithms after automatic robotic palpation. Then for learning algorithms, a training data was generated by [Nichols et al. (2019)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_037) using specific characterization of elastography and noninvasive and inclusion of tissue biomechanics. A stiffness of artificial tissue was characterized using elastography along with an embedded hard inclusion and after identification of inclusion on the elastography image, the difference in stiffness between surrounding soft tissue and inclusion was identified using ML methods. The stiffness values were labeled using it by being either the part of inclusion or soft tissue. Further through artificial tissues of automatic robotic palpation, a stiffness distributed map was created over the tissue surface. The main motive behind it to find the thresholder against the ML algorithm trained classifiers and label the hard inclusion belonged point theorized. The lack of feedback force was the main limitation of robot-assisted minimally invasive surgeries and by simply adding sensors will not be a practical solution in clinical environment. Due to this, [Aviles et al. (2015)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_007) have presented a force estimation approach, first, by minimizing an energy functional to reconstruct 3D deformation structure of the tissue surface. Then they have presented the architecture on the basis of recurrent NN-long–short-term memory that gives accurate estimation. The proposed approach is tested that shows a long-term stability is achieved along with a significant improvement in accuracy percentage that ranges from 54% to 78% over existing approaches.

### 5.4.5 ML in personalized healthcare

A new healthcare approach is personalized healthcare or PH that improves the traditional healthcare system by mainly focusing on Internet of things sensor devices, web-based or social media information, and patient electronic health records collected patients’ data. Then by PH, an AI technique was applied on collected data that helps in improving the clinical intervention, disease progression, prediction of diseases, and patient self-management. Then to make analytic models, ML techniques that were used were integrated into different clinical decision support system and healthcare service applications. From sensor devices, a collected data was analyzed using these models, and behavioral pattern of patients along with their clinical conditions was identified through other sources.

For classification purpose, either supervised or unsupervised ML was used. Sometimes the decisions made by humans can be wrong due to involvement of emotion; on the other hand, computers do not have emotions. So, people are biased in decision making on the basis of their perception toward political, religious, and world. In the available dataset used for training purpose, these factors can create a problem as it was used for training it to make decision. Due to all these factors, the Esurance of dataset that was used for ML becomes challenging. Overcoming these will result in enhancement of personal healthcare. [Ahamed et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_001) have worked on ML models that were used in recent research and identified its reliability issues and proposed an improvement. For continuous monitoring of everyone health by using biomedical devices and prediction of potential health hazards, a PH plays a crucial role. And if these hazards were not treated on time, then it can become dangerous. [Mr. Walinjkar et al. (2017)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_034) have mainly focused on constant monitoring of ECG signal of an individual; for this purpose, a 3-lead ECG kit was used and arrhythmia was detected by performing real-time analysis. This enables the prediction of heart risk and at ML phase a classification accuracy was needed to be dealt with as a feature extraction method was developed. This method was able to increase the classification accuracy. For development of prediction models, classification, and learning development, an arrhythmia database (MIT-BIH, Physionet) was used. The proposed models were tested and its results show that an accuracy of 99.4% was achieved using *k*\-NN classifier model along with the cross-entropy error of 7.6 and on test data there was 1.2 of misclassification error.

### 5.4.6 Discovery of drug prediction using ML

Various researchers have worked on orthogonal technique in which through identification of ligands that are nondockable, search space is reduced using soft computing. This will improve the throughput; on the other hand, the binary classifier can be trained using ML that classifies ligands into dockable and nondockable, the two known classes. Due to it [Senanayake et al. (2013)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_039) have used random forest, ANN, and SVM ML techniques on single problem domain. The problem domain was a protease receptor of HIV and evaluated its performance that shows an improvement of 90% accuracy in throughput by twofold. They have also proposed and used techniques for construction of training set that proved to be useful for ML in instances of a nonsynthesized receptor. The identification of disease-associated proteins affected candidate molecules is a main problem in drug discovery. Number of ML predictor programs was used for experiment purpose in which new candidates were derived from the chemogenomic projects of Inside Janssen Pharmaceutica. The programs were written in single-node C++ that takes more time in running and are inherently parallel but multiple nodes are not used in it. [Harnie et al. (2015)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_019) have showed that how pipeline is reimplemented using Apache spark that enables lifting of existing programs into multinode clusters. In spark pipeline benchmark and predictors, no changes were done against the original that helps in linear speedup of up to eight nodes. Along with it, their pipeline was able to generate fewer intermediate files and allow easier monitoring and check pointing.

## 5.5 Deep learning in healthcare

Another name of deep learning is a hierarchical deep structured learning, which is a division of ML ([Hinton, 2006](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_023)). These AIs emulating inspired algorithms developed a layered hierarchical architecture of learning and data representation. It is also inspired from neocortex in the deep human brain, and layered learning process is able to extract abstractions and features from data automatically. In greedy layer-wise methods when one has to deal with a large amount of unsupervised data and natural learn data representation, then deep learning algorithms give improved results ([Bengio, 2009](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_009)). Deep learning has found its use in various applications like image recognition, biometric, NLP, and medical healthcare. The main motive of this section is to provide the usage of deep learning in various healthcare applications.

### 5.5.1 Deep learning solutions in healthcare and other industries

Various deep learning patterns and hidden chances were used for treating a patient. From the last few years, a lot of attention has been gained by AI and ML but now even deep learning has created its own path in various fields or industries like tourism, retail, textiles, tourism, and healthcare ([Bengio, 2013](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_010)). The main concern of every person in today’s modern world is their health; that’s why various technologies like deep learning were implemented in health industries. Various ways have been proposed by medical experts for implementation of new technologies that should be able to give good results for better future ([Arel, 2010](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_006)). The deep learning is able to put a large amount of data including patient’s records along with medical reports, insurance reports, and personal data, which help in giving better treatment for a good outcome.

### 5.5.2 Deep learning in robotic surgery

Due to faster rate of recovery and less pain, laparoscopy is used for minimally invasive surgery in various clinical surgeries. There is very narrow operating space and field of view that is insufficient to tactile feedback that can damage surgical instruments, organs, and tissues. So, during laparoscopic surgery for detection of surgical instruments, a real-time model on the basis of convolutional neural network (CNN) was used [Choi et al. (2017)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_013). For testing purpose, they have used a dataset of seven surgical tools for learning CNN, then a YOLO unified architecture is used to track surgical instruments in real time. To evaluate a proposed model, a precision and a degree of recall are used for performance that shows an average of 72.26% of accuracy over dataset. It has been predicted that semantic segmentation was a very big problem for robotic instruments surgery in which correct detection of position of instrument was the main challenge for pose tracking and estimation in the vicinity of surgical scenes. In order to address these challenges, there is a need of accurate pixel-wise instrument segmentation; so, for robotic instrument segmentation, [Shvets et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_041) have proposed a deep learning-based approach that addresses the problem of binary segmentation. Through a feed from surgery video every pixel of images was labeled as instrument or background and also addresses multiclass segmentation problem, in which they had differentiated between different parts and different instruments from the background. The proposed method is tested and compared with other methods for automatic instrument segmentation that gives improvement.

### 5.5.3 Deep learning in drug discovery

By an increase in drug candidates, there is an increase in social burden of drug discovery. In drug response vitro models, a traditional preclinical drug scene was not able to mimic the complex properties of human biology. Due to this there is need to be contributed. The Gregor Urban et al., has proposed a new micro-physiological system for growing perfused micro-tissues that seems to be suitable for large drug screens and also able to models human physiology accurately. Then in 2018, Gregor U., et al., (2018) have developed a ML model that is able to accurately and quickly predict flag commands that were able to disrupt vascular networks effectively from drug application before and after images in vitro. The proposed system is based on CNN that is able to achieve high or 98.1% of accuracy without putting any false negative effects on system.

In traditional drug discovery, there is problem of cost and time so there is a need to propose a new method that will increase its efficiency. To solve this problem, use of virtual screening (VS) and its database has been a famous method for pharmaceutical research. VS plays a very important role in early stages of drug development and discovery as it helps in reducing the enormous search space of chemical compounds. For effective distinguishing, active and inactive legends are used due to an increase in the count of ligands by time. For big database molecules a screening was done using deep learning algorithms that further classify druglike and nondruglike ligands against a particular protein target. This will result in an increase of VS process speed. [Bahi et al. (2018)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_008) have proposed a deep NN-based fast compound classification method for VS that is known as DNNVS. Then from a huge database, a Spark-H2O platform was used to label small molecules. The proposed approach that is evaluated shows that the accuracy achieved using DNNVS is 99% and performed well when compared with ML techniques.

### 5.5.4 Deep learning in disease diagnosis

Breast cancer has become a leading cause of death among women; and for diagnosis and screening of breast cancer, a mammogram is used as a standard. To standardize mammographic reporting, a breast imaging reporting and data system (BI-RADS) lexicon has been developed by the American College of Radiology that enables the decision-making facility of biopsy and assesses cancer risk. Due to the use of inappropriate terms and missing data, unstructured and semistructured free text were made for current biopsy decision making due to variation in their accuracy. Due to BI-RADS lexicon application and occurrence of substantial interobserver variability, a variation takes place that makes critical to incorporate an accurate and novel technique for breast cancer decision-making data. Then to develop an analytic model, [He et al. (2017)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_021) have combined deep learning methods and NLP that target well-defined and characterized specific breast-suspicious patient subgroups. This model is able to give better results when compared with SVM, Gaussian mixture model, and PCA algorithm. [Tourassi (2017)](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_ref_043) has discussed both theoretical and practical perspectives of the latest deep learning technology that is relevant to clinical pathology NLP reports. Various information extraction tasks of benchmark studies were presented and their importance was discussed in supporting a scalable and comprehensive national cancer surveillance program ([Tables 5.1](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_tab_001) and [5.2](#bodymatter-005.xhtml#b_9783110648195-005_tab_002)).

## 5.6 Current and future directions of deep learning in healthcare and medical domains

The current and future applications of deep learning in healthcare are evident and can be easily observed. Some of them are:

*   In healthcare industry, a transfer of knowledge taken from heterogeneous, complex, and high-dimensional biomedical information is a challenging task. In modern world of medical science, there is abundant amount of data like data of sensor text, electronic records, imaging, etch that has been solved to much extension with the use of deep learning algorithms. A complex or unstructured example can be represented successfully with the use of deep learning, and an efficient and new paradigm is obtained using latest deep learning technology that is able to obtain an end-to-end learning model for complex types of data.
    
*   In advance clinical research, a promising result is obtained using electronic health records and it also better informs the clinical decision-making skills. With modern electronic health records, a predictive modeling can be prevented with a practice of summarizing and representation. The use of alternative feature learning strategy has proved to be successful in patients who have achieved better results using electronic health record data. From various studies done by different researchers, it can be said that use of deep learning framework arguments in clinical environment decision systems gives improved results.
    
*   In thrombosis, use of urokinase and plasminogen activator streptokinase and therapy can no longer be an approach as approved by the Food and Drug Administration. Instead of it, a deep learning comes into existence for the treatment of acute peripheral arterial thrombosis and embolism along with acute coronary thrombosis.
    
*   There are some measures and outcomes of deep learning trained algorithms. In detection, a reference diabetic retinopathy sensitive and specificity nature of the algorithm can be defined as worst or moderate one. Because of this reason, two development set-selected operating points are used in deep learning trained algorithms. In this the first operating point handles high specificity and another one handles high sensitivity that results in better results.
    
*   For identification of relevant information from patient records and charts, a deep learning NN can be used. As a scalable and accurate prediction can be created for variety of clinical resource sceneries, a deep learning approach can be used.
    
*   It is also been used for analyzing medical images and in future it can also be used in medical segmentation, classification, registration, and various other tasks. Deep learning has been used in medical fields such as neural, digital pathology, pulmonary, and retinal.
    
*   In predictions, a better result has been achieved using deep learning algorithm developed data. Hence, a very important tool of ML in computer domains such as vision and imaging has been developed.
    

## 5.7 Conclusion

A large amount of data has been used by industrial organizations and recognized using ML. By collecting perceptions from these data a more efficient work can be done by organization and gets better results than their competitors. In various domains, a use of ML algorithms has been observed in innovative predictive models. Even deep learning techniques are getting used in various day-to-day activities like advertisements, searching, and YouTube. The use of ML and deep learning techniques in the field of multidisciplinary and healthcare informatics has become the same in terms of data-handling challenges and technological advertisements. There are large numbers of ML algorithms and further they have been classified into four broad categories by considering their nature of learning that is explained in Section 5.2. Supervised learning, unsupervised learning, semisupervised learning, and RL are four ML methods. Section 5.3 includes a systematic procedure to use ML in healthcare. Due to storage and generation of large amounts of data, a use of ML is getting increased in enterprise predictions. In order to have high accuracy and value to enterprise datasets, an effective framework, applications, and algorithms are provided by ML. With rapid growth of population, it becomes challenging to analyze and record the large amount of available data of patients. The healthcare systems become more robust and dynamic with the help of ML that automates the processing and find out data from large database. Medical science and computer science are two domains that come in single thread using ML in healthcare. The use of ML techniques helps in advancement of medical science and analyzing the complex medical data for further analysis. Various researchers have worked on the number of healthcare applications where ML was used. Along with ML, data learning is also being used in medical and healthcare. Another name of deep learning is a hierarchical deep structured learning that is a division of ML. Section 5.4 described deep learning solutions in healthcare and other industries, and a comparison study on work done by various researchers in the field of medical or healthcare using deep learning algorithm. Further, the current and future applications of deep learning in healthcare are evident and can be easily observed as given in Section 5.6.
